F_{h}(s):=-s\left(\big(\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{k=1}^{h}b_{k}-(\max_{i=1}^{m}e_{i})J_{\xi}(s)\big)\right)

has negative derivative and hence
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
is a decreasing function in
n
.
For
n/D<\xi
and
n/D\in[u_{h},u_{h+1}]
, we have

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})=-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|+D\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}+\sum_{k=h+1}^{r}u_{k}b_{k}\right)+\left(\sum_{k=1}^{h}b_{k}\right)n.

Since
\sum_{k=1}^{h}b_{k}\leq\sigma_{m}<\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|
, we conclude that
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
is a decreasing function.

Thus we obtain (
7.7.3
), giving a proof of Theorem
7.0.1
free of appeal to the Shidlovsky type theorems from §
3.2
, but under the stronger assumption that
\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|>\sigma_{m}+\max_{i=1}^{m}(e_{i})
. In particular, in a manner
free of any of the references in §
3
, these remarks already suffice for proving Theorem
2.5.1
except for the clause that
|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|>e^{\max(\sigma_{m},\tau(\mathbf{b}))}
can be relaxed to
|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|>e^{\sigma_{m}}
when the
f_{i}
are
a priori
supposed holonomic.

7.7.5.
Discussion for the
\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{0}
case of Theorem
7.1.6

As the behavior of the function
J_{\xi}
is the main obstacle to devising a clean proof of our general holonomy bounds not relying on functional bad approximability theorems for holonomic functions, and since we do not logically need an alternative proof for any of our applications, we are content
(still in the context of explaining how to bypass §
3.2
)
for Theorem
7.1.6
with demonstrating how to handle the case
\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{0}
and under the seemingly mild extra condition that

m\geq\max_{0\leq k\leq l}\left\{\frac{\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{L}}\right)-\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{r_{k}}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{L}}\right)+\alpha_{k}\left(\log|\varphi^{\prime}_{r_{k}}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{h(k)}b_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=h(k)+1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}}{\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sigma_{m}}\right\},

(7.7.6)

where, for a given
k
, we pick the
h(k)
with
\alpha_{k}\in[u_{h(k)},u_{h(k)+1})
, and we recall the convention
\alpha_{0}=0
.
In other words, we will show without appealing to §
3.2
that when
\mathbf{e}=\mathbf{0}
and under the conditions of Theorem
7.1.6
, at least one of the dimension bounds (
7.1.8
) or

m\leq\max_{0\leq k\leq l}\left\{\frac{\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{L}}\right)-\left(\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{r_{k}}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{L}}\right)+\alpha_{k}\left(\log|\varphi^{\prime}_{r_{k}}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{h(k)}b_{j}\right)-\sum_{j=h(k)+1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}}{\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sigma_{m}}\right\}

(7.7.7)

is in place.
In practice, we have always found that the inequality (
7.7.7
) is already implied by the contrapositive of the condition inequality (
7.1.8
), in which case
the conclusion (
7.1.8
) certainly follows.

For
n/D\in[\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k+1}]\cap[u_{h},u_{h+1}]
(here
h
does not need to be
h(k)
defined above), the proof of Theorem
7.1.6
shows that

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})=-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-n\log r_{k}+D(\overline{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{L}}_{r})+n\sum_{j=1}^{h}b_{j}+D\sum_{j=h+1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j},

(7.7.8)

which, if viewed as a piecewise linear function in
s=n/D
, is continuous on
s\in[0,\infty)
. The local minima
can only occur at points of the form
s=\alpha_{k}
, or over a line segment of slope
0
.
But for
n/D\geq m
we use as archimedean height evaluation bound

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})=-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|+D(\overline{\mathcal{L}}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{L}})+n\sigma_{m},

which decreases monotonically in
s=n/D
. Now to show (
7.7.3
), which as in (
7.7.1
) suffices to bypass the appeal to §
3.2
in our analysis in §
7.4
, it is enough to check that

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(mD)})\leq\min_{0\leq k\leq l}\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(\alpha_{k}D)});

here as
\alpha_{k}D
might not be an integer, by a slight abuse of notation,
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(\alpha_{k}D)})
means replacing
n
in (
7.7.8
) by
\alpha_{k}D
.
This unfolds to the definition of the condition (
7.7.6
).

7.7.9.
Discussion for Theorem
7.1.13

Under the conditions

\displaystyle\xi>u_{1}\geq 1,\quad b_{1}

\displaystyle>\log R\geq 0,\quad\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|>\sigma_{m}+\max_{1\leq i\leq m}e_{i},

(7.7.10)

\displaystyle\Gamma(\log R,r,\lambda,\mu)

\displaystyle\geq u_{1}\log R,

we show independently of §
3.2
that at least one of the dimension bounds (
7.1.14
) or

m\leq\frac{T(\varphi;r,\lambda,\mu)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}}{\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sigma_{m}-(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}e_{i})J_{\xi}(m)}

(7.7.11)

is in place.
In the practical situations of many applications, usually (
7.7.11
) is expected to be a smaller bound than (
7.1.14
). This applies for example to our proof of Theorem
A
via Example
7.5.9
, where the conditions (
7.7.10
) are met, and the right-hand side of (
7.7.11
) is negative, whereas the holonomy bound of (
7.1.14
) is at
\sim 13.8527
.

Let
\overline{h}_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
and
\overline{h}_{\mathrm{fin}}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
denote the respective main terms of our bounds on
h(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
,
given in (
7.5.5
), (
7.5.4
), and (
7.5.6
) (for the archimedean estimates), and (
7.3.13
) and (
7.3.11
) (for the finite estimates), according to the various cases in dependence on
n/D
.
In these notations we have
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
continuous in
s:=n/D
, and hence again we only need to ensure (
7.7.3
).

In the case at hand, our assumptions imply
m\geq\max\{\chi_{1},\xi,u_{1},\ldots,u_{r}\}
. For
n\geq mD
, we derive for the left-hand side of (
7.7.3
):

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})=-n\left(\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sigma_{m}-\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}e_{i}\right)J_{\xi}(n/D)\right)+DT(r,\lambda,\mu).

By the same analysis as in §
7.7.1
, we derive that
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
is a decreasing function of
n
in the range
n\geq\max\{\xi,\chi_{1}\}D
; therefore for
n\geq mD
, we have

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})\leq\min_{\max\{\xi,\chi_{1}\}D\leq n^{\prime}<mD}\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)}).

It remains to consider the range
n^{\prime}<\max\{\xi,\chi_{1}\}D
.
If
\xi>\chi_{1}
, then for
s^{\prime}:=n^{\prime}/D\in[\chi_{1},\xi]\cap[u_{h},u_{h+1}]
, we have

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})=-n^{\prime}\left(\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{h}b_{j}\right)+D\left(T(r,\lambda,\mu)+\sum_{j=h+1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}\right),

and therefore
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})
is a decreasing function in
s^{\prime}
in the range
[\chi_{1},\xi]
;
continuity the gives

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})\leq\min_{\chi_{1}D\leq n^{\prime}<mD}\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)}).

Next we take up the range
\xi\leq s^{\prime}<\chi_{1}
. Here we have for
s^{\prime}\in[u_{h},u_{h+1}]
,

\displaystyle\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})=

\displaystyle n^{\prime}\left(-\log R+\sum_{j=1}^{h}b_{j}+\left(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}e_{i}\right)J_{\xi}(n/D)\right)

\displaystyle+D\left(\Gamma(\log R,r,\lambda,\mu)+\sum_{j=h+1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}\right).

Since
u_{1}<\xi
, we have
h\geq 1
for these
n^{\prime}
and then
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})
is an increasing function of
s^{\prime}
due to our assumption
\log R<b_{1}
(while, by definition,
J_{\xi}\geq 0
).

We continue: for
u_{1}\leq s^{\prime}<\min\{\xi,\chi_{1}\}
(if
u_{1}>\chi_{1}
, then this set is empty and move on to the next case below), we have (the
h
in the formula may vary depending on
s^{\prime}
as above)

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})=n^{\prime}(-\log R+\sum_{j=1}^{h}b_{j})+D\left(\Gamma(\log R,r,\lambda,\mu)+\sum_{j=h+1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}\right),

also an increasing function of
s^{\prime}
.

For
\chi_{0}\leq s^{\prime}<\min\{u_{1},\chi_{1}\}
,

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})=-n^{\prime}\cdot\log R+D\left(\Gamma(\log R,r,\lambda,\mu)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}\right),

is a decreasing function of
s^{\prime}
due to our assumption
\log R\geq 0
.

Finally, for
0\leq s^{\prime}<\chi_{0}
, we have

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})=D\left(-\lambda(s^{\prime})^{r}-\mu s^{\prime}+\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}\right).

Recall that
\lambda>0
. If
\mu>0
, then
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})
is a decreasing function. If
\mu\leq 0
,
the critical point
s_{0}
satisfies that

s_{0}=\left(\frac{-\mu}{r\lambda}\right)^{1/(r-1)}\leq\left(\frac{\log R-\mu}{r\lambda}\right)^{1/(r-1)}=\chi_{0},

under our assumed conditions;
therefore
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})
is an increasing function on
[0,s_{0}]
and a decreasing function on
[s_{0},\chi_{0}]
.

The above discussion shows that

\min_{0\leq n^{\prime}\leq mD}\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n)})=\min\{\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(0)}),\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(u_{1}D)}),\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(mD)})\}.

Since

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(0)})=D\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j},\,\,\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(u_{1}D)})=-u_{1}D\log R+D\left(\Gamma(\log R,r,\lambda,\mu)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}\right),

we have
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(0)})\leq\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(u_{1}D)})
due to the assumption
\Gamma(\log R,r,\lambda,\mu)\geq u_{1}\log R
.

In upshot, the requisite inequality

\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(mD)})\leq\min_{0\leq n^{\prime}<mD}\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(n^{\prime})})

boils down to securing that
\overline{h}(\psi_{D}^{(mD)})\leq D\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}
, which is equivalent to

m\geq\frac{T(r,\lambda,\mu)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}u_{j}b_{j}}{\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sigma_{m}-(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}e_{i})J_{\xi}(m)}.

In other words, we have either proved (
7.7.3
) and hence (
7.1.14
) holds, or else (
7.7.11
) holds.

8.
The finer holonomy bound with the Bost–Charles integral

This section combines the measure concentration input of §
6
with the Bost–Charles refinements of §
7
to give the most accurate general holonomy bound of all the theorems worked out in our paper. The added strengthening turns out to be zero for the particular applications to Theorems
A
and
C
, and the theorem becomes somewhat complicated to state, nevertheless we hope that the principle of the abstract refinement could be useful in future applications of our holonomy bounds.

The theoretical improvement from adding high-dimensional methods to the Bost–Charles calculus is, as far as we were able to tell in the framework of §
2
, reflected only in the denominator term
\tau(\mathbf{b;e})
. It is inevitable to ask which of the growth integrals in §
6
versus §
7
is the smaller.
In §
8.1
, we present a proof by Fedor Nazarov that the Bost–Charles integral is strictly better than the rearrangement integral.
This, in particular, implies that Theorem
7.6.4
is more precise than Theorem
6.0.2
, granting our heuristic Remark
7.6.7
, and at least as far as the stated denominator is concerned in the latter theorem. (Remark
6.6.15
indicates that the multidimensional proof in §
6
can go further than §
7
in the general denominator aspect, and at least as far as our choice of treatment in the present section §
8
.) Remark
8.1.17
further down in this section suggests that the difference in the archimedean growth terms is usually very small, implying that little is to be lost from working with the rearrangement integrals. For purposes of sampling and testing the holonomy bounds with different maps
\varphi
, the latter integrals have the practical advantage to allow for faster and more reliable numerical computations.

We now proceed to formulating our unifying theorem.
The following sums up the sharpest
32
32

32

Possibly up to considering other
\varphi
in addition to
\varphi_{r}
, as in Theorems
6.0.2
or
7.1.13
. Such variations are straightforward to incorporate as well, but we refrain from doing this here.
of all the holonomy bounds we prove in this paper.

Theorem 8.0.1
.

We relax all assumptions on the denominators in Theorem
6.0.2
, and allow for an
arbitrary denominators matrix
\mathbf{b}\in M_{m\times r}\left(\mathbf{R}_{\geq 0}\right)
with nonnegative coefficients.
Define

\displaystyle\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})

\displaystyle:=\limsup_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0,\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{1}{d}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathrm{Den}_{N}(\mathbf{b},\epsilon,d,\varepsilon)\right\},

(8.0.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Den}_{N}(\mathbf{b},\epsilon,d,\varepsilon)

\displaystyle:=\frac{2}{N}\sum_{k\in\mathbf{N}_{>0}}\max_{\mathbf{n}\in P_{\varepsilon}^{d}(N),\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\#\{(j,h)\,:\,k\leq b_{i_{j},h}\cdot n_{j}\},

where
(j,h)
runs through
\{1,\ldots,d\}\times\{1,\ldots,r\}
,
the set
V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}^{d}
is defined as

\displaystyle V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon):=\big\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}^{d}\,:\,\forall i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

\displaystyle d/m-\epsilon d<\#\{1\leq j\leq d\mid i_{j}=i_{0}\}<d/m+\epsilon d\big\},

and

P_{\varepsilon}^{d}(N)\subset[0,N]^{d}\cap\mathbf{Z}^{d}
denotes the subset of those
\mathbf{n}
for which the normalized
\left([0,1),\mu_{\mathrm{Lebesgue}}\right)
discrepancy of
\{n_{i}/N\}_{i=1}^{d}
is
\leq\varepsilon
.

Assume either that

\displaystyle{\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|>\max\left\{\sum_{h=1}^{r}\max_{1\leq i\leq m}b_{i,h},\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})+\tau^{\sharp}(\mathbf{e})\right\}}
, or that
\log{\varphi^{\prime}(0)}>,\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})+\tau^{\sharp}(\mathbf{e})
and all
f_{i}
are holonomic.

Then we have

m\leq\frac{\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)}{\log{|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|}-(\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})+\tau^{\sharp}(\mathbf{e}))}.

(8.0.3)

Further, for any subradii sequences
1=r_{l}>r_{l-1}>\cdots>r_{0}>0
as in Theorem
7.1.6
and
using the Bost–Charles characteristic
\widehat{T}
of Definition
7.1.2
, we have

m\leq\frac{\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)-\frac{1}{m}\sum_{k=1}^{l}\frac{(\widehat{T}(r_{k},\varphi)-\widehat{T}(r_{k-1},\varphi))^{2}}{\log{r_{k}}-\log{r_{k-1}}}}{\log{|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|}-(\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})+\tau^{\sharp}(\mathbf{e}))}.

(8.0.4)

Moreover, if the
s_{h}^{*}
for the given
\{r_{k}\}
are defined as in Theorem
7.6.4

then we have

m\leq\frac{\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)-\sum_{h=0}^{l-1}s_{h}^{*}\cdot(\widehat{T}(1,\varphi)-\widehat{T}(r_{h},\varphi))}{\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-(\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})+\tau^{\sharp}(\mathbf{e}))}.

(8.0.5)

Remark 8.0.6
.

We observe that
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})\in[0,\infty)
by definition. More precisely, we have the trivial bound
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})\leq\sum_{h=1}^{r}\max_{i=1}^{m}\{b_{i,h}\}
.

For the denominator matrices
\mathbf{b}
of the form considered throughout §§
6
–
7
, we have
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})=\tau^{\flat}(\mathbf{b})
. Indeed, writing
t
for the continuous limit of the discrete variable
k/N
,
we have in the setup of Theorem
6.0.2

\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})
bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{h=1}^{r}\limsup_{\epsilon,\varepsilon\rightarrow 0}\limsup_{d\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{1}{d}\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{2}{N}\sum_{k\in\mathbf{N}_{>0}}\max_{\mathbf{n}\in P_{\varepsilon}^{d}(N),\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\#\{(j,h)\,:\,k\leq b_{i_{j},h}\cdot n_{j}\}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{h=1}^{r}\int_{0}^{b_{h}}\min\{1-u_{h}/m,1-t/b_{h}\}\,dt=\sum_{h=1}^{r}\left(b_{h}-\frac{b_{h}u_{h}^{2}}{m^{2}}\right)=\tau^{\flat}(\mathbf{b}),

where the inequality stems from the observation that the restriction to the
balanced

\mathbf{i}
(meaning: each
i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
occurs with the same asymptotic frequency
1/m
) supplies the upper bound constraint
1-u_{h}/m
in the integrand on the second line, while the restriction to the
balanced

\mathbf{n}
(meaning: the components set
\{n_{j}\}
takes asymptotically the uniform distribution on
[0,N]
) supplies the upper bound constraint
1-t/b_{h}
in that integrand. Moreover, equalities are reached in the case where both
\mathbf{n}
and
\mathbf{i}
are arranged in non-decreasing order:
n_{1}\leq\cdots\leq n_{d}
and
i_{1}\leq\cdots\leq i_{d}
. This
proves that
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})=\tau^{\flat}(\mathbf{b})
under the standing assumptions throughout §
6
and §
7
. (See also Lemma
6.6.7

and Remark
6.6.14
.)

\triangle

Example 8.0.7
.

Here is a simple example to illustrate that, for a given
\mathbf{b}
,
if one lets
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
range over all arrays which dominate
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}\geq\mathbf{b}
coefficient-wise
and which additionally meet the constraints of
Theorem
6.0.2
, the inequality in
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})\leq\tau^{\flat}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})
can be strict.

Consider
\mathbf{b}=[0,1,2]^{\mathrm{t}}
. In order to use
\tau^{\flat}
, the optimal choice of
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}
is to take
\mathbf{b}^{\prime}=[0,2,2]^{\mathrm{t}}
and we have
\tau^{\flat}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=\frac{16}{9}
. On the other hand, we have
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})=\frac{5}{3}=\frac{15}{9}
. Indeed, writing
t:=k/N
, we have:

\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})=2\left(\int_{0}^{2/3}\frac{2}{3}\,dt+\int_{2/3}^{1}\left(1-t+\frac{1}{3}\right)\,dt+\int_{1}^{4/3}\frac{1}{3}\,dt+\int_{4/3}^{2}\left(1-\frac{t}{2}\right)\,dt\right).

This is to be compared with

\tau^{\flat}(\mathbf{b}^{\prime})=2\int_{0}^{2}\min\left\{\frac{2}{3},1-\frac{t}{2}\right\}\,dt.

To explain the difference between two formulas, we notice that in the range
t\in[2/3,4/3]
, for every
\mathbf{n}
to be considered in the definition of
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})
, there are at most
(\max\{0,1-t\}+o(1))d
among the
n_{j}
with
i_{j}=2
(corresponding to
b_{2,1}=1
) to contribute to
\{(j,1)\mid k\leq n\}
, and there are at most
(1/3+o(1))d
among the
n_{j}
with
i_{j}=3
(corresponding to
b_{3,1}=2
) to contribute to
\{(j,1)\mid k\leq 2n\}
and both bounds can be reached with suitable choice of
\mathbf{n}
.

\triangle

8.1.
Comparison of the Bost–Charles and the Rearrangement integrals

This section, due entirely to Fedor Nazarov, treats the clean comparison of the two integrals — beneath the empirical observation that they are practically the same in the situations
we encounter in §
A
, as well as in practice for most of the multivalent cases. This theorem strictly speaking is not used for any of our proofs in the paper,
and hence it can be omitted on a first (and on a second) reading.

Basic Remark 8.1.1
.

As the considerations that follow rely on the potential theory in the plane, consider first the easier situation on the simplest of all the Lie groups: the circle
\mathbf{T}
.
The integrable function
G(z):=\log{\frac{1}{|1-z|}}
,
G:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{R}\cup\{\infty\}
has the nonnegative Fourier coefficients

\widehat{G}(n):=\int_{\mathbf{T}}G(z)z^{-n}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)=\int_{\mathbf{T}}z^{-n}\log{\frac{1}{|1-z|}}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0,&\textrm{ if }n=0;\\
\displaystyle{\frac{1}{2|n|}},&\textrm{ if }n\neq 0.\end{array}\right.

(8.1.2)

By the general Bochner theorem, the positivity of the Fourier transform implies that
G(z)
is
a positive-definite function on the locally compact abelian group
\mathbf{T}
: that is,

\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}G(zw^{-1})\,\nu(z)\nu(w)\geq 0
for all
reasonable
signed measures
\nu
on
\mathbf{T}
.
Reasonable

here may be taken to mean
\nu=\nu^{+}-\nu^{-}
with finite positive measures
\nu^{\pm}
satisfying
\int_{\mathbf{T}}G\,\nu^{\pm}<\infty
.
For any such signed measure
\nu
, this computation shows more precisely that

\displaystyle I(\nu)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{\frac{1}{|z-w|}}\,\nu(z)\nu(w)

(8.1.3)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{\frac{1}{|1-zw^{-1}|}}\,\nu(z)\nu(w)=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}\smallsetminus\{0\}}\frac{|\widehat{\nu}(n)|^{2}}{2|n|}\geq 0,

where manifestly the equality holds if and only if the Fourier transform
\widehat{\nu}
is a scalar multiple of the Dirac mass
at
0\in\widehat{\mathbf{T}}=\mathbf{Z}
, and that in turn is the case if and only if
\nu
is a scalar
multiple of the Haar measure
\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}
.
This reflects the basic potential theory on the circle.

On the circle
|z|=R
of arbitrary radius, the left-hand side of (
8.1.3
) scales by the additive summand
(\log R)\left(\int\nu\right)^{2}
, and so whereas for
R>1
the inequality (
8.1.3
) is false for arbitrary measures
\nu
supported by that circle, it continues to be in place for the measures that are
balanced
in the
sense that
\int\nu=\nu(\mathbf{C})=0
. As the logarithmic kernel is also invariant under additive translations, the latter remark continues to hold for balanced
measures carried by any circle in
\mathbf{C}
.

\triangle

As we review next, the positivity of the energy integral is a completely general fact about balanced measures on
\mathbf{C}
.

8.1.4.
The energy principle

In the Newtonian gravitational field created by a point mass at the origin
\mathbf{0}\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
, the potential energy function
U:\mathbf{R}^{n}\smallsetminus\{\mathbf{0}\}\to\mathbf{R}
is determined by the distributional Laplace equation

\Delta U:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\partial^{2}U}{\partial x_{i}^{2}}=-\delta_{\mathbf{0}},

namely as the fundamental solution
U:=U_{n}^{2}
of that equation, where more generally it is useful to consider the
Riesz potential

[
Rie38
]

defined by

U_{n}^{\alpha}(\mathbf{x}):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n}{2}\right)}{2\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\log{\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}\|}}},&\,{\textrm{ for }}n=\alpha;\\
\displaystyle{\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{n-\alpha}{2}\right)}{2^{\alpha}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)\pi^{\frac{n}{2}}}\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x}\|^{n-\alpha}}},&\,\textrm{ for }n>\alpha>0.\end{array}\right.

(8.1.5)

Here,
\|\mathbf{x}\|:=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}^{2}}
is the Euclidean distance function in
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. The gravitational potential
U=U_{n}^{2}
is rotationally invariant
and defines a kernel function
k(\mathbf{x,y}):=U_{n}^{2}(\mathbf{x-y})
, which by definition is furthermore translationally invariant.
For
n>2
this kernel is also positive-definite, by the fundamental formula of Frostman and Marcel Riesz
[
Rie38
, § I.3]
, which generalizes
the Dirichlet energy integral
\frac{1}{2}\iint\|\nabla F\|^{2}\,\mathrm{dvol}
, and is tantamount
to the computation
[
Den50
,
Sch66
,
Lan72
,
NS91
]
of the distributional Fourier transform of
U_{n}^{2}
:

\displaystyle I(\nu)

\displaystyle:=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}k(\mathbf{x,y})\,\nu(\mathbf{x})\nu(\mathbf{y})=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}U_{n}^{2}(\mathbf{x-y})\,\nu(\mathbf{x})\nu(\mathbf{y})

(8.1.6)

\displaystyle

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\left(U_{n}^{1}\ast\nu\right)^{2}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Lebesgue}}.

This is the spatial analogue of the energy formula (
8.1.3
) for the circle.
To be more precise, this formula gives the strict
positivity
I(\nu)>0

of the energy
of any nonzero compactly supported signed measure
\nu=\nu^{+}-\nu^{-}
on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
expressible as the difference of two finite positive Borel measures

\nu^{+},\nu^{-}
of finite energies
I(\nu^{+}),I(\nu^{-})<\infty
.
For the logarithmic kernel (the case
n=2
, whose proper physical interpretation is rather in electrostatics on a plate), Riesz observed
[
Rie38
, § I.4]

that the analogy becomes almost perfect upon additionally requiring the signed measure
\nu
to be
balanced
:
\nu(\mathbf{R}^{2})=0
.
The
energy principle
states that for balanced signed measures subject to the above regularity conditions (with the balancing condition being
only required in the case
n=2
), the energy
I(\nu)\geq 0
is nonnegative, and equality holds if and only if
\nu=0
. This refines the uniqueness theorem for
the equilibrium probability measure of a compact.
The
n=2
case, which is the one of relevance to us, is treated in detail in
[
Hil62
, Theorem 16.4.2]
.

8.1.7.
Fuglede’s inequality

For completeness, we summarize the more general situation due to Fuglede
[
Fug60
]
for non-balanced measures. This is
a different generalization of the
n=2
case, this time to the logarithmic kernel

k(\mathbf{x,y}):=U_{n}^{n}(\mathbf{x-y})=\frac{\Gamma(n/2)}{2\pi^{n/2}}\,\log\frac{1}{\|\mathbf{x-y}\|}

on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. For this kernel, the analog of the energy formula (
8.1.6
) for the case of the balanced measures is also due to Riesz
[
Rie38
, § I.4]
:

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\nu=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad I(\nu)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\left(U_{n}^{n/2}\ast\nu\right)^{2}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Lebesgue}}\geq 0,

and more generally,
Fuglede
[
Fug60
, § 4]
proves the sharp energy lower bound

I(\nu)\geq\log{\left(\frac{a_{n}}{R}\right)}\cdot\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\nu\right)^{2}

(8.1.8)

for all signed measures
\nu
on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
expressible as
\nu=\nu^{+}-\nu^{-}
with
\nu^{\pm}
finite positive measures of convergent energy
integrals
I(\nu^{\pm})<\infty
and having
\mathrm{supp}(\nu)\subseteq\{\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq R\}
, and with the optimal constant
a_{n}
being precisely

a_{n}:=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\displaystyle{\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}+\ldots+\frac{1}{n-4}+\frac{1}{n-2}\right)},&\textrm{ for $n$ even};\\
\displaystyle{\exp\left(\frac{1}{1}+\ldots+\frac{1}{n-4}+\frac{1}{n-2}-\log{2}\right)},&\textrm{ for $n$ odd}.\end{array}\right.

The cases
a_{1}=1/2
and
a_{2}=0
(for the case
n=2
of relevance to us) are already in de la Vallee-Poussin
[
dlVP49
, § 47]
. In any case, (
8.1.8
)
certainly implies the requisite positivity
I(\nu)\geq 0
for all (reasonable)
balanced
measures, and more generally, for all measures supported by a sufficiently small ball.

8.1.9.
Michelli’s criterion for positive-definite kernels

A different generalization, for which we refer to
[
Mat97
]
and the references there, admits an arbitrary kernel of the form
k(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=U\left(\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\right)
, where
U(t)\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathbf{R}_{>0}\right)
obeys
(-1)^{n}\left(\frac{d}{dt}\right)^{n}U(t)\geq 0
for all
n\geq n_{0}
, and now the additional (“balancing”) constraints on the compactly supported signed measure
\nu=\nu^{+}-\nu^{-}
with
I(\nu^{\pm})<\infty
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
being
\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}}\,\nu(\mathbf{x})=0
for all
\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}
with
|\mathbf{m}|<n_{0}
. The condition on
U(t)
is equivalent to the existence of an integral representation
for
\left(\frac{d}{dt}\right)^{n_{0}}U(t)=\int_{0}^{\infty}e^{-tu}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha(t)
as a Laplace–Stieltjes transform of a
positive
Borel measure
\mathrm{d}\alpha
on
\mathbf{R}_{>0}
.

8.1.10.
Intersection pairing and signature

Another way of informally summarizing this discussion
33
33

33

This is taking
n_{0}:=1
in §
8.1.9
if we are to include the more general kernels
k(x,y)
there. For arithmetic geometry, the case of relevance is
n=2
and the kernel
k(z,w)=-\log{|z-w|}
.
is to say that
the infinite-dimensional quadratic form

\langle\mu,\nu\rangle:=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}k(\mathbf{x,y})\,\mu(\mathbf{x})\nu(\mathbf{y})

has one ‘
-
’ sign on the space of
reasonable
(non-balanced) signed measures on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. For the case
n=2
of relevance
to the rest of §
8.1
, it could be interesting to know if a more precise connection could be drawn to the arithmetic Hodge index
formula in Arakelov theory and the computations in
[
BC22
, § 5]
that led to the Bost–Charles double integral. Is there a proof of Nazarov’s inequality (Proposition
8.1.13
below) that works directly into the arithmetic intersection theory framework of
[
BC22
]
?
A basic remark in the algebraic model is that for any two line bundles
L,M
on a polarized normal projective algebraic surface, if

(L.L)=(M.M)
and
\deg{L}=\deg{M}
, then
(L.L)\leq(L.M)
following from the Hodge index theorem for
the line bundle
L\otimes M^{-1}
.

8.1.11.
Nazarov’s inequality

Consider now the case
n=2
as the complex plane
\mathbf{R}^{2}\cong\mathbf{C}
. Then the rotation group
is realized by the unitary transformations
t_{a}\,:\,z\mapsto az
, indexed by the circle points
a\in\mathbf{T}
,
and our rotationally-invariant, positive-definite kernel is given by
k(z,w)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\log{\frac{1}{|z-w|}}
on the
\mathbf{C}
-linear space
of balanced signed measures on
\mathbf{C}
with the regularity conditions we described. We consider a non-constant continuous
function
\varphi:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{C}
, and for any
a\in\mathbf{T}
we apply the energy principle
I(\nu)\geq 0
to the balanced signed measure

\nu=\nu_{a}:=\varphi_{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}})-t_{a}^{*}\varphi_{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}})
. As the kernel
k(z,w)
is symmetric and rotationally invariant, the resulting inequality rewrites as

\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)\leq\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{|a\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w),

(8.1.12)

for any
a\in\mathbf{T}
, and with equality holding if and only if
\nu_{a}=0
. Integrating over
a\in\mathbf{T}
we get:

Proposition 8.1.13

(Nazarov)
.

For any continuous function
\varphi:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{C}
,

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log\max\left(|\varphi(z)|,|\varphi(w)|\right)\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}2t\cdot(\log{|\varphi(e^{2\pi it})|})^{*}\,dt,

where
g^{*}
denotes the increasing rearrangement (
2.4.1
) of a continuous function
(0,1)\to\mathbf{R}
.
Furthermore, equality holds if and only if
\varphi(z)=cz^{m}
for some
c\in\mathbf{C}
and
m\in\mathbf{Z}
.

Proof.

Using the Poisson formula

\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log{|ax-y|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a)=\log\max{(|x|,|y|)}

in the termwise integration
\int_{\mathbf{T}}I(\nu_{a})\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(a)\geq 0
of (
8.1.12
). The equality requires
\nu_{a}=0
for almost all
a\in\mathbf{T}
, hence that

\varphi_{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}})
is rotationally-invariant, and hence that the supporting loop
\varphi(\mathbf{T})\subset\mathbf{C}
is rotationally-invariant and therefore
a centered circle, and that
\varphi^{*}(\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}})
is a scalar multiple of the Haar measure of that circle.
∎

In particular, we get a clean proof that the Bost–Charles integral is always strictly majorized by the doubled Nevanlinna characteristic that we
have in
[
CDT24
]
(and not merely by the slightly larger doubled Ahlfors–Shimizu characteristic

\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log{\sqrt{1+|\varphi|^{2}}}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

noted in
[
BC22
, Prop. 5.4.5]
, which is a more basic estimate following simply by the trivial pointwise inequality
|x-y|^{2}\leq(1+|x|^{2})(1+|y|^{2})
):

Corollary 8.1.14
.

Every continuous function
\varphi:\mathbf{T}\to\mathbf{C}
satisfies

\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)\leq 2\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log^{+}{|\varphi|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}.

Figure 8.1.15
.
The plots of the Rearrangement (in solid) and the Bost–Charles integrals (dashed) for the bivalent function
\varphi_{r}(z)=(rz)-(rz)^{2}
. Above them (dotted),
the plot of
2\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log^{+}{|\varphi_{r}|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}
.

Example 8.1.16
.

Consider the function
\varphi_{r}(z):=rz-(rz)^{2}
with the varying radius
r
.
For
r\geq 1
,
the Bost–Charles integral amounts to

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{|\varphi_{r}(z)-\varphi_{r}(w)|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(w)

\displaystyle=2\log{r}+\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log^{+}{\left|\frac{r}{1-rz}\right|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)

\displaystyle=2\log{r}+\frac{1}{\pi r}+\frac{1}{72\pi r^{2}}+\ldots.

In comparison, a computation reveals the
rearrangement integral as the explicit function

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}2t\cdot(\log{|\varphi_{r}(e^{2\pi it})|})^{*}\,dt=2\log{r}+

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2\pi^{2}}\left(8\,\mathrm{Li}_{3}\left(1/r\right)-\mathrm{Li}_{3}\left(1/r^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=2\log{r}+\frac{4}{\pi^{2}r}+\frac{4}{27\pi r^{2}}+\ldots.

The comparison is pretty tight for most values of
r
, as illustrated by Figure
8.1.15
.
In contrast, the Nevanlinna characteristic upper bound by

\displaystyle 2\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log^{+}{|\varphi_{r}|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

\displaystyle=2\int_{|z|=r}\log^{+}{|z-z^{2}|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z)

\displaystyle=4\log r\ \text{when}\ r\geq\frac{\sqrt{5}+1}{2}

is quite
crude.

\triangle

Remark 8.1.17
.

The asymptotic equivalence of the two growth characteristic integrals at a “big” radius
|z|=r

(as observed in
Example
8.1.16
and Figure
8.1.15
) seems to be a fairly general feature
that reflects the near-rotational invariance of the
\varphi_{*}
pushforward of the uniform measure
\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}
of the expanding circle
|z|=r
, considering
the proof of Proposition
8.1.13
via the slice-by-slice inequality (
8.1.12
). An example “in action” is in our proof
of Theorem
C
in §
14.5
. In this situation, the Bost–Charles integral (
A.6.1
) compares
with the rearrangement integral (
A.6.3
) as follows:

9.963\sim\begin{aligned} \iint_{\mathbf{T}^{2}}\log{|\varphi(z)-\varphi(w)|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(z,w)\leq\int_{0}^{1}2t\cdot(\log{|\varphi(e^{2\pi it})|})^{*}\,dt\,\sim 9.972,\end{aligned}

an improvement on the order of merely a tenth of a percent. (In contrast, the Nevanlinna characteristic quantity
2T(\varphi)=2\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log^{+}{|\varphi|}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}
from (
B.0.1
) is in this case as big as
\sim 14.08
.)

Another example
of this kind can be seen by comparing
the upper bounds occurring in the two proofs of Theorem
A

coming from Theorem
6.0.2
and Theorem
7.6.4

respectively. The proof via Theorem
6.0.2
(with two division points,
see §
13
)
gives a bound in terms of rearrangement
integrals of the form
m<13.731
.
On the other hand,
Theorem
7.6.4

gives
a bound in terms of arithmetic intersection numbers
which are expressible as integrals which are slight generalizations
of the Bost–Charles integrals (see Lemma
7.4.5
).
With an analogous choice of division points, this leads to the bound
m<13.679

(see Example
7.6.8
, in particular Equation
7.6.9
),
an improvement of less than half a percent.
This comparison is not literally an example
of Proposition
8.1.13
because of the slightly modified forms of both
the integrals and the bounds
arising from the convexity argument. However, it accurately reflects
the amount of improvement between the
rearrangement
integrals and Bost–Charles integrals that
we observed numerically
without convexity.

\triangle

8.2.
Proof of Theorem
8.0.1

We firstly prove (
8.0.4
), which contains (
8.0.3
) as a special case. We will discuss at the end how to modify the proof to get (
8.0.5
).

For the following, we fix an
\epsilon>0
and the number of variables
d
.
Only at the end of the proof we will let, firstly,
d\rightarrow\infty
, followed by
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.
To compare to §
6
and (
6.0.10
), we remark that the bounds in Theorem
8.0.1
only have
\tau^{\flat\kern-1.2pt\flat}(\mathbf{b})
in the denominator using a high dimensional equidistribution feature, while the other terms are the same as those in the one dimensional bounds in §
7
. Therefore, to prove Theorem
8.0.1
, we only need to incorporate the feature of a balanced index
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})
in
\prod_{j=1}^{d}f_{i_{j}}(x_{j})
, while we do not need an equidistributed degree
\mathbf{k}
in
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{k}}
as in §
6.2
. This motivates the following choice
for the Euclidean lattice to underlie our auxiliary evaluation module.

8.2.1.
The Euclidean lattice

Consider the free
\mathbf{Z}
-module

E_{D}:=\bigoplus_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}f_{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbf{x})\,\mathbf{Z}[1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{d}]_{\leq D},

(8.2.2)

where we recall that
V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)\subset\{1,\ldots,m\}^{d}
is defined (depending on the fixed small positive constant
\epsilon
) as

\displaystyle V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)=\big\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}\in\{1,\ldots,m\}^{d}\,:\,\forall i_{0}\in\{1,\ldots,m\},

\displaystyle d/m-\epsilon d<\#\{1\leq j\leq d\mid i_{j}=i_{0}\}<d/m+\epsilon d\big\};

and, as usual,
f_{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbf{x}):=\prod_{j=1}^{d}f_{i_{j}}(x_{j})
. Here,
\mathbf{Z}[1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{d}]_{\leq D}
denotes the free
\mathbf{Z}
-module consisting of integer polynomials in
1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{d}
, all of whose partial degrees — with respect to each
1/x_{j}
— are at most
D
.

By construction,
\mathrm{rank}\,E_{D}=(D+1)^{d}\cdot\#V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)
. By Theorem
4.2.1
(actually, the weak law of large numbers suffices here),
we have

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}{m^{d}}=1,\text{ thus }\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\mathrm{rank}\,E_{D}}{m^{d}D^{d}}=1.

In order to endow
E_{D}
with the suitable norm, we consider the smooth projective arithmetic scheme
\mathcal{X}:=(\mathbf{P}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{1})^{d}
and the natural very ample line bundle
\mathcal{L}:=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\,\mathcal{O}(1)
on
\mathcal{X}
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{j}:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Z}}
denotes the projection onto the
j
-th component.
Then we can identify
\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})
with
\mathbf{Z}[1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{d}]_{\leq D}
, where
x_{j}:=Y_{j}/Z_{j}
is an affine coordinate of the
j
-th
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Z}}=\mathrm{Proj}\,\mathbf{Z}[Y_{j},Z_{j}]
.

Recall that we are given a holomorphic map
\varphi:(\overline{\mathbf{D}},0)\rightarrow(\mathbf{P}^{1}(\mathbf{C}),0)
, where “
0
” can mean
x_{j}=0
for each copy of
\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{C}}
in
\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{C}}
.
The Bost–Charles metric from §
7.3.1
is thus defined using
\varphi
on every factor
\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\,\mathcal{O}(1)
.
This induces a Hermitian line bundle structure
\overline{\mathcal{L}}=\left(\mathcal{L},\|\cdot\|_{\overline{\mathcal{L}}}\right)
on
\mathcal{L}
, and in turn, as in §
7.3.3
, a Euclidean lattice

\Gamma_{L^{2}}\left(\mathcal{X},\nu;\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)
after we fix a smooth probability measure
\nu
on
(\mathbf{P}^{1})^{d}(\mathbf{C})
. The choice of
\nu

is immaterial to the proof, since
D\to\infty
for the fixed
\nu
and we only need to study the asymptotic leading order term given by the arithmetic
Hilbert–Samuel formula for
\widehat{\deg}\,\,\Gamma_{L^{2}}\left(\mathcal{X},\nu;\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)
. For concreteness, we pick
\nu
to be the smooth measure

\nu:=\bigwedge_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\,\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}=\left(\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\right)^{d}\frac{dz_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dz_{d}\wedge d\bar{z_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge d\bar{z_{d}}}{\prod_{j=1}^{d}(1+|z_{j}|^{2})^{2}}

with
\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}=\frac{\sqrt{-1}}{2\pi}\frac{dz\wedge d\bar{z}}{(1+|z|^{2})^{2}}

being the Fubini–Study form on
\mathbf{P}^{1}(\mathbf{C})
.
Then, as in §
7.3.3
, the Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|
on
\Gamma\left(\mathcal{X},\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)
is defined by

\|s\|:=\sqrt{\int_{\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{C})}\|s\|_{\overline{\mathcal{L}}}^{2}\,\nu}.

(8.2.3)

Just as in §
7.3.3
, we take the orthogonal direct sum (
8.2.2
) of the Euclidean lattices

\mathbf{Z}[1/x_{1},\ldots,1/x_{d}]_{\leq D}\cong\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})\subset\Gamma\left(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}\right)_{\mathbf{R}}

induced from the above norm (
8.2.3
) on
\Gamma\left(\mathcal{X},\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)
. We use
\overline{E}_{D}=\left(E_{D},\|\cdot\|\right)
to denote this Euclidean lattice.

8.2.4.
Arithmetic degree

The asymptotic calculation of the arithmetic degrees of direct images to
\mathrm{Spec}\,{\mathbf{Z}}
is the subject of the arithmetic Hilbert–Samuel formula:

Lemma 8.2.5
.

As
D\rightarrow\infty
, we have the following asymptotics of arithmetic degrees:

\displaystyle\widehat{\deg}\,\Gamma_{L^{2}}\left(\mathcal{X},\nu;\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{2}\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\right)D^{d+1}+o(D^{d+1}),

(8.2.6)

\displaystyle\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{E}_{D}

\displaystyle=\frac{d\#V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}{2}\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\right)D^{d+1}+o(D^{d+1}).

Proof.

As Euclidean lattices,
\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D})\cong\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\Gamma\left(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Z}},\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}^{\otimes D}\right)
, where the norm on each factor

\Gamma\left(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Z}},\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}^{\otimes D}\right)
is the
L^{2}
-norm using the Hermitian line bundle
\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}
and the Fubini–Study form
\omega_{\textrm{FS}}
on
\mathbf{P}^{1}(\mathbf{C})
. Therefore, by Lemma
7.2.7
, we have

\widehat{\deg}\,\Gamma\left(\mathcal{X},\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)=d(D+1)^{d-1}\widehat{\deg}\,\Gamma\left(\mathbf{P}^{1}_{\mathbf{Z}},\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}^{\otimes D}\right).

Now the first assertion follows from the arithmetic Hilbert–Samuel formula (
7.3.4
).
The second assertion follows from the first one by (
7.2.4
)).
∎

Remark 8.2.7
.

A proof of this calculation is also a consequence of the general arithmetic Hilbert–Samuel formula
in Krull dimension
d+1
. In

[
Zha95
, Theorem 1.4]
, the Hilbert–Samuel formula is proven for an arithmetic variety of any dimension and an ample Hermitian line bundle with smooth metric of pointwise non-negative Chern form. Using the idea in
[
Bos99
, §5]
and
[
BC22
, §§3–4]
, the same formula continues to hold for an ample line bundle with a
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}
Hermitian metric of pointwise non-negative Chern form over an arithmetic variety of any dimension.
Thus we may also deduce Lemma
8.2.5
directly from the arithmetic Hilbert–Samuel formula for
(\mathcal{X},\overline{\mathcal{L}})
:

\widehat{\deg}\,\Gamma_{L^{2}}\left(\mathcal{X},\nu;\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{\otimes D}\right)=\frac{\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{d+1}}{(d+1)!}D^{d+1}+o(D^{d+1}).

Here,
\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{d+1}
denotes the arithmetic self-intersection number
\widehat{c}_{1}(\overline{\mathcal{L}})^{d+1}.[\mathcal{X}]
. In our situation, we write
\overline{\mathcal{L}}=\otimes_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}
, and expand the self-intersection number by multilinearity. The only nonzero terms come from crossing all but one of the
\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}
factors once and the remaining
\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}
factor twice.
By the projection formula, that gives us

\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{d+1}=d\binom{d+1}{2}(d-1)!\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\right),

and we recover Lemma
8.2.5
by this perspective also.

\triangle

8.2.8.
Evaluation filtration

Writing
X:=\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Q}}
, we can identify
\mathrm{Spf}\,\mathbf{Q}\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket=\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}
, giving in particular elements

f_{\mathbf{i}}=f_{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbf{x})\in\Gamma(\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}}).

The space
\Gamma\left(\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}\right)
of global sections of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}|_{\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}}
is then naturally identified with

\Gamma\left(\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}\right)=\mathbf{x}^{-D}\mathbf{Q}\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket=:F_{\mathbf{Q}},

where
\mathbf{x}^{-D}\mathbf{Q}\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket
denotes the
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space generated by the
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{k}}
with
k_{j}\geq-D
for all
1\leq j\leq d
. Thus
f_{\mathbf{i}}\,\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})\subset\Gamma(\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})
, and we have the injective evaluation map

\psi_{D}:E_{D}\hookrightarrow F_{\mathbf{Q}},\quad\left(Q_{\mathbf{i}}\right)_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\mapsto\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}f_{\mathbf{i}}Q_{\mathbf{i}},

where
Q_{\mathbf{i}}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})
and
(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\in E_{D}
.

Similarly to §
3.1.3
, we filter
F_{\mathbf{Q}}
using the total vanishing order and then the lexicographical ordering within every jet space:

F_{\mathbf{Q}}=F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{0})}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}\supseteq\cdots,

where
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}
, and
F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}:=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbf{Q}}\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}}\,:\,\mathbf{n}\prec\mathbf{m}+D\text{ or }\mathbf{n}=\mathbf{m}+D\}
.
Here, the total order
\prec
on
\mathbf{N}^{d}
is defined in §
3.1.3
, and for
\mathbf{m}=(m_{j})_{j=1}^{d}
, we define
\mathbf{m}+D:=(m_{j}+D)_{j=1}^{d}
. The ordering
x_{1},\ldots,x_{d}
of the variables used to define the lexicographical ordering is immaterial to the proof.
In this
\prec
-filtering notation, since
\prod_{j=1}^{d}x_{j}^{-D}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})
is a generator of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}_{\mathbf{0}}
, where we use
\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{0}}
to denote the restriction of
\mathcal{L}
to the
\mathbf{Z}
-point
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{0}
, we observe that the
\mathbf{x=0}
vanishing order

\mathrm{ord}_{\mathbf{0}}(g(\mathbf{x}))
of a
g(\mathbf{x})\in F_{\mathbf{Q}}=\Gamma(\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D})
(as a regular section of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}|_{\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}}
, not as a Laurent series in
\mathbf{x}^{-D}\mathbf{Q}\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket
) is at least
n
if and only if
g(\mathbf{x})\in F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(0,\ldots,0,n)}
.
We also observe that
g(\mathbf{x})\in F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}
if and only if either
\mathrm{ord}_{0}(g(\mathbf{x}))>|\mathbf{n}|
or else
\mathrm{ord}_{0}(g(\mathbf{x}))=|\mathbf{n}|
and the lowest lexicographical order term in the homogenous degree
|\mathbf{n}|
part in
g
has an exponent vector
\mathbf{m}
such that
\mathbf{n}\prec\mathbf{m}
.
(If here one prefers to think of
g
as a Laurent series in
\mathbf{x}^{-D}\mathbf{Q}\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket
rather than as a section of

\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}|_{\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}}
, one would have to shift all exponent vectors
\mathbf{n}
to
\mathbf{n}-D
in these statements.)

As in §
3.1.3
, we use
\mathbf{n}^{+}
to denote the successor of
\mathbf{n}
under the total order
\prec
. The graded piece
F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}/F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}
is a one dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space generated by the image of
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{n}-D}
under the quotient map. The Euclidean lattice structure on
F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}/F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}
is given by the free rank one
\mathbf{Z}
-module generated by the image of
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{n}-D}
and the Euclidean norm with
\|\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{n}-D}\|=1
. Note that these Euclidean lattice structures on graded piece are all induced from the free
\mathbf{Z}
-module
F=\mathbf{x}^{-D}\,\mathbf{Z}\llbracket\mathbf{x}\rrbracket
and with the Euclidean norm that has
\{\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}}\}_{\mathbf{m}\in\mathbf{Z}_{\geq-D}^{d}}
for an orthonormal basis.

Let
{E}^{(n)}_{D}:=\psi_{D}^{-1}\left(F^{(n)}_{\mathbf{Q}}\right)\cap E_{D}
denote the preimage of
F^{(\mathbf{n})}_{\mathbf{Q}}
in
E_{D}
under
\psi_{D}
.
Then
\psi_{D}
induces injective maps

\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}:{E}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{D}/{E}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}_{D}\hookrightarrow F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}/F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}.

into the one-dimensional
\mathbf{Q}
-vector space
F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n})}/F_{\mathbf{Q}}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}
.
Therefore
\mathrm{rank}\,{E}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{D}/{E}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}_{D}\in\{0,1\}
for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}
. Let

\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}:=\{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}\mid\mathrm{rank}\,{E}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{D}/{E}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}_{D}=1\}

be the vanishing filtration jumps. We have
\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}=\mathrm{rank}\,E_{D}
.

In the next two subsections §
8.2.11
and §
8.2.29
, we provide an upper bound on the local evaluation heights
h_{v}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})
at all
v\in M_{\mathbf{Q}}
.
From the definition of the local evaluation height, we need to consider an arbitrary
(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\in{E}^{(\mathbf{n})}_{D}\smallsetminus{E}^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}_{D}
and then provide an upper bound on
\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|_{v}-\log\|(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\|_{E_{D},v}
, where
c_{\mathbf{n}}
denotes the coefficient of
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{n}-D}
in
s:=\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}f_{\mathbf{i}}Q_{\mathbf{i}}
. Here,
|\cdot|_{v}
denotes the usual
v
-adic norm on
\mathbf{Q}
.

Definition 8.2.9
.

For a formal power series
F(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d})\in k\llbracket t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}\rrbracket=\sum_{\mathbf{n}}a_{\mathbf{n}}\mathbf{t^{n}}
over a field
k
, the
n^{\mathrm{th}}
order jet
of
F

is the degree-
n
homogeneous polynomial
J_{n}(F)\in k[t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}]_{(n)}
given by the sum of all the degree-
n
terms:

J_{n}(F)(t_{1},\ldots,t_{d}):=\sum_{|\mathbf{n}|=n}a_{\mathbf{n}}\mathbf{t^{n}}\in k[\mathbf{t}]_{(n)}.

Remark 8.2.10
.

In earlier work
[
Bos01
,
Bos04
]
on the algebraization of higher dimensional formal-analytic arithmetic varieties,
it sufficed to filter the auxiliary evaluation module using only the total vanishing order at the point
\mathbf{0}
. That we use the finer filtration with one-dimensional quotients has two advantages:

(1)

For the estimate of
h_{v}
at an archimedean place
v
, using the product structure of
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{C})
, we have an easy “variable by variable” subharmonic estimate similar to
[
CDT21
, Lemma 2.4.1]
.
This obviates the blowing-up method in
[
Bos01
, §4.3.2]
, which gives an upper bound on
the Mahler measure of the leading order jet polynomial
J_{n}(F)\in\mathbf{Q}[\mathbf{x}]_{(n)}
, whereas the quantities
that need to be estimated are the individual coefficients
c_{\mathbf{n}}
in that leading order jet. The discrepancy in these quantities is too sensitive in the
dimension
d=\dim{\mathcal{X}_{\mathbf{Q}}}
, which is fixed in
[
Bos01
]
whereas we want to have
d\to\infty
at the end.

(2)

The advantage for the
h_{v}
estimate at a non-archimedean place
v
is crucial. Among all
\mathbf{n}
with
|\mathbf{n}|=n
, due to our specific construction of
E_{D}
, we will have a much better estimate of
h_{v}\left(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}\right)
under the condition that
\{n_{j}\}_{j=1}^{d}
has asymptotically equidistributed components. Our complete filtration with including the lexicographical ordering allows us to take stock of this improvement.

\triangle

8.2.11.
Archimedean estimate

Recall that by the same reduction argument as in the beginning of the proof of Lemma
7.4.1
, we may assume that
f_{i}(\varphi(z))
is meromorphic on an open neighborhood of
|z|\leq 1
, for all
1\leq i\leq m
.

For ease of notation, we use
|\cdot|
to denote the usual absolute value
|\cdot|_{\infty}
. The broader outline of the proof is similar to
[
Bos01
, §4.3.3]
, with caveat the modification we described in Remark
8.2.10
(
1
). Given
s\in\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})}(E^{\mathbf{n}}_{D}\smallsetminus E^{\mathbf{n}^{+}}_{D})
, we will follow
[
CDT21
, § 2.4]
in studying the
n^{\textrm{th}}
(leading) order jet
J_{n}\left(\varphi^{*}s\right)(\mathbf{z})\in\mathbf{C}[\mathbf{z}]_{(n)}

at the point
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{0}\in\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
. Here and in the following, we use the notation
n:=|\mathbf{n}|
, and by a slight abuse of notation, we continue to denote
\varphi:\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{C})
for the analytic morphism
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\left(\varphi(z_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(z_{d})\right)
given by
\varphi:\overline{\mathbf{D}}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}

diagonally on each factor. By extension of that notation, and in a manner unifying §
6
and §
7.4
, we consider for every
\mathbf{r}\in(0,1]^{d}
the
analytic morphism

\varphi_{\mathbf{r}}:\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}\to\mathbf{C}^{d}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{C}),\qquad\mathbf{z}\mapsto\left(\varphi(r_{1}z_{1}),\ldots,\varphi(r_{d}z_{d})\right).

We will use the Poisson–Jensen formula to bound
\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|
in terms of the jet function, then relate the resulting bound to the Chern form of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
by means of the Poincaré–Lelong formula. We follow the notations in §
7.4
,
and we borrow from (
7.1.7
) the notation for the slopes
\alpha_{k}
. In addition, for each
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}
, we define
r(\mathbf{n}):=\left(r(n_{1}),\ldots,r(n_{d})\right)
, where

r(t):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}r_{k},&\quad\textrm{if }t/D\in[\alpha_{k},\alpha_{k+1}),\\
1,&\quad\textrm{if }t/D>m.\end{array}\right.

(8.2.12)

Throughout this section,
\mathbf{z}=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{d})
denotes the coordinate on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
. We use the trivialization
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}|_{\mathbf{C}^{d}}\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbf{C}^{d}}
of
\mathcal{L}^{\otimes D}

over
\mathbf{A}_{\mathbf{C}}^{d}
given by

\mathbf{x}^{-D}\mapsto\mathbf{1}
. Under this identification,
s/\mathbf{x}^{-D}=s\cdot\mathbf{x}^{D}=:G(\mathbf{x})
is naturally in
\Gamma(\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}},\mathcal{O}_{\widehat{X}_{\mathbf{0}}})
with vanishing order
n
.
Since our analytic
\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})}
-pullback is defined by
x_{j}=\varphi_{r(n_{j})}(z_{j})=(\varphi^{\prime}(0)\cdot r(n_{j}))z_{j}+O(z_{j}^{2})
, we have by
construction that
\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G
has
\mathbf{z}=\mathbf{0}
vanishing order
n
, with
c_{\mathbf{n}}\varphi^{\prime}(0)^{n}\prod_{j=1}^{d}r(n_{j})\,\mathbf{z}^{\mathbf{n}}
for the
lexicographically minimal term in
J_{n}(\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})}^{*}G)
(as well as the overall
\prec
-minimal term in
\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})}^{*}G
).
However, since we only assume in this theorem the
meromorphy
(as opposed to the holomorphy) of
the pullbacks:
\varphi^{*}f_{i}\in\mathcal{M}(\overline{\mathbf{D}})
, the analytic germ
\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\in\mathbf{C}\llbracket\mathbf{z}\rrbracket
only extends meromorphically, rather than holomorphically through
\overline{\mathbf{D}}
. But if we choose
h\in\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{D}})
a holomorphic function such that
h(0)=1

and all
h\cdot\varphi^{*}f_{i}\in\mathcal{O}(\overline{\mathbf{D}})
are holomorphic, then
h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\left(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\right)G(\mathbf{z})\in\mathcal{O}\left(\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}\right)
is holomorphic throughout
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
, and has the same leading order jet
J_{n}\left(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right)
as
\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G
.

This puts us in a position to use
[
CDT21
, Lemma 2.4.1]
for upper-bounding the requisite coefficient
|c_{\mathbf{n}}|
in terms of the Mahler measure of the
(homogeneous) polynomial
J_{n}\left(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right)=J_{n}\left(h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right)
, in which certainly the overall lexicographically lowest term is the monomial
c_{\mathbf{n}}\mathbf{z}^{\mathbf{n}}
in the multidegree
\mathbf{n}
:

\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|\leq-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})+\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\left|J_{n}\left(h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right)\right|\,\mu_{\rm{Haar}}.

(8.2.13)

We can connect this to Bost’s blowing up argument in
[
Bos01
, Equation (4.28) in Lemma 4.13]
which shows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\left|J_{n}\left(h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right)\right|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\left|h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

(8.2.14)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\left|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}+O_{h}(1).

To recall Bost’s argument, write
h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot G(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}(\mathbf{z}))=:\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}}c^{\prime}_{\mathbf{k}}\mathbf{z}^{\mathbf{k}}
, and define

U_{t}(\mathbf{z}):=\sum_{\mathbf{k}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}}c^{\prime}_{\mathbf{k}}t^{|\mathbf{k}|-n}\mathbf{z}^{\mathbf{k}},\quad\textrm{ for $t\in\mathbf{C}$ with }|t|\leq 1.

(8.2.15)

Then, by the
\mathbf{z}\mapsto t\mathbf{z}
substitution,

\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log|U_{t}(\mathbf{z})|\,\mu_{\rm{Haar}}(\mathbf{z})=\int_{|t|\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\left|h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})}^{*}G\right|\,\mu_{{\rm Haar}}(\mathbf{z})-n\log|t|.

(8.2.16)

In particular,
\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log|U_{t}(\mathbf{z})|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(\mathbf{z})
,
which by (
8.2.15
) is clearly a subharmonic
function in the single complex variable
t
, only depends on that complex variable
t
through
|t|
by means of the right-hand side of the identity (
8.2.16
). Hence the value of that function at
t=1
, which equals
\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\left|h(z_{1})\cdots h(z_{d})\cdot\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G\right|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

but is also the
t\in\mathbf{T}
integral of
\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log|U_{t}(\mathbf{z})|\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}(\mathbf{z})
, is no less than its value at
t=0
, which is the left-hand side of the requisite bound (
8.2.14
).

Next we follow the proof idea of
[
Bos20
, Theorem 10.5.3]
, along with the discussion in
[
BC22
, §§4.2–4.3]
in order
to pass from smooth Green functions to
\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{b}\Delta}
ones.
By the product structure of
\overline{\mathcal{L}}
and the computation from the proof of Lemma
7.4.1
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\mathbf{x}^{-D}\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\int_{\mathbf{T}}\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}(x_{j}^{-D})\|_{\overline{\mathcal{O}(D)}}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

(8.2.17)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(-\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log^{+}|z|^{-1}\,c_{1}\left(\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}\overline{\mathcal{O}(D)}\right)+\|\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}(x^{-D})\|_{\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}\overline{\mathcal{O}(D)}}\mid_{z=0}\right).

Since
x^{-D}\mid_{x=0}
is a
\mathbf{Z}
-generator of the free
\mathbf{Z}
-module
\mathcal{O}(D)_{0}
, we have

\|\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}(x^{-D})\|_{\overline{\mathcal{O}(D)}}\mid_{z=0}=-\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(D)}_{0}=-D\,\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}_{0}.

Putting together (
8.2.13
), (
8.2.14
), and (
8.2.17
), we have

\displaystyle\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|-\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}s\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}

(8.2.18)

\displaystyle\leq-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\left(\log|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G(\mathbf{z})|-\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}s\|_{\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}}}\right)\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}+O_{h}(1)

\displaystyle=-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})-\int_{\mathbf{T}^{d}}\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\mathbf{x}^{-D}\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}\,\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}+O_{h}(1)

\displaystyle=-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})

\displaystyle+D\left(\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{\deg}\,(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}_{0})+\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log^{+}|z|^{-1}c_{1}(\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)})\right)+O_{h}(1)

\displaystyle=-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})+D\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi)+O_{h}(1),

where
\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))
is the Bost–Charles characteristic we defined in
7.1.2
, and the final equality derives from the projection formula
[
BC22
, Proposition 7.2.2]
applied to the morphism
(\iota,\varphi_{r(n_{j})})
in Remark
7.3.2
and Lemma
7.4.5
. We spell out that last step in more detail. Following the definitions of pullback of Hermitian vector bundles in
[
BC22
, §7.1.1.1]
and the arithmetic intersection number in
[
BC22
, Equation 6.2.4]
, we have

(\iota,\varphi_{r(n_{j})})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],\log^{+}|z|^{-1})=\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}_{0}+\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log^{+}|z|^{-1}c_{1}(\varphi^{*}(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)})).

By the projection formula
[
BC22
, Proposition 7.2.2]
together with Lemma
7.4.5
, we derive the requisite equality

(\iota,\varphi_{r(n_{j})})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],\log^{+}|z|^{-1})=\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi)),

and the final line on (
8.2.18
) follows.

We claim that (
8.2.18
) implies for an arbitrary
(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\in E^{(\mathbf{n})}_{D}\smallsetminus E^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}_{D}
a uniform upper bound:

\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|-\|(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\|\leq-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})+D\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))+o(D).

(8.2.19)

As
\mu_{\mathrm{Haar}}
is not a continuous measure on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
, we begin by approximating
\log^{+}|z|^{-1}
by a sequence
(g_{k})_{k\in\mathbf{N}}
of smooth
rotationally symmetric Green functions on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}
for the divisor
[0]
. Precisely, by
[
Bos20
, page 268]
,
we choose
g_{k}\in C^{\infty}(\overline{\mathbf{D}})
with
\mathrm{supp}(g_{k})\subset\mathbf{D}
, such that
g_{k}(z)=g_{k}(|z|)
and

g_{k}-\log^{+}|z|^{-1}\to 0
uniformly on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}
.
Following the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
7.4.1
(see, for instance,
[
Bos20
, pages. 270–271]
for the one-dimensional case), we write
\frac{i}{\pi}\partial\overline{\partial}g_{k}=-\delta_{0}+\mu_{k}
, where
\mu_{k}
is a smooth probability measure on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}
, and then, denoting by
\mu_{k}^{d}
the product measure induced from
\mu_{k}
on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
:

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}}\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\mathbf{x}^{-D}\|_{\varphi^{*}_{(r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}\,\mu_{k}^{d}

\displaystyle=-D\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}g_{k}c_{1}(\varphi^{*}_{r(n_{j})}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)})+\widehat{\deg}\,\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}_{0}\right)

\displaystyle=-D\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\iota,\varphi_{r(n_{j})})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],g_{k}).

Moreover, since
g_{k}
and
\mu_{k}
are rotationally invariant, Poisson–Jensen gives:

\displaystyle\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|+n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|+\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}}\left(\log|J_{n}(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G)(\mathbf{z})|-\log|\mathbf{z}|^{\mathbf{n}}\right)\mu_{k}^{d}

\displaystyle=\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}}\log|J_{n}(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G)|\,\mu_{k}^{d}-n\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log|z|\mu_{k};

and our previous argument gives

\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}}\log|J_{n}(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G)|\,\mu_{k}^{d}\leq\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}}\log|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}G|\,\mu_{k}^{d}.

Since
\mu_{k}
is smooth on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
and
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
is compact, there exists a constant
C_{k}>0
depending only on

d,g_{k},\varphi,\mathbf{r}
(but independent of
n,D
, and
s
), such that
C_{k}\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\nu=C_{k}\bigwedge_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{pr}_{j}^{*}\varphi_{r(n_{j})}^{*}\,\omega_{\mathrm{FS}}\geq\mu_{k}^{d}
as a pointwise inequality for smooth
(d,d)
-forms on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\log\|(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\|

\displaystyle\geq\max_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\log\|Q_{\mathbf{i}}\|=\frac{1}{2}\max_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\log\int_{\mathcal{X}(\mathbf{C})}\|Q_{\mathbf{i}}\|_{\overline{\mathcal{L}}}^{2}\,\nu

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\log C_{k}+\frac{1}{2}\max_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\log\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}}\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}Q_{\mathbf{i}}\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}^{2}\,\mu_{k}^{d}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{1}{2}\log C_{k}-\log\left\{m^{d}(D+1)^{d}\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq m,\\
z\in\overline{\mathbf{D}}\end{subarray}}|f_{i}(z)|^{d}\right\}+\frac{1}{2}\log\int_{\mathbf{D}^{d}}\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}s\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}^{2}\,\mu_{k}^{d}

\displaystyle>-C_{k}^{\prime}-d\log D+\frac{1}{2}\log\int_{\mathbf{D}^{d}}\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}s\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}^{2}\,\mu_{k}^{d}

\displaystyle\geq-C_{k}^{\prime}-d\log D+\int_{\mathbf{D}^{d}}\log\|\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}s\|_{(\varphi^{*}_{r(\mathbf{n})}\overline{\mathcal{L}})}\,\mu_{k}^{d},

where
C_{k}^{\prime}>0
is a constant only depending on
d,m,\{f_{i}\},g_{k},\varphi,\mathbf{r}
(but independent of
n,D
, and
s
), and the last inequality follows from
the quadratic mean — geometric mean inequality since
\mu_{k}^{d}
is a probability measure on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}^{d}
(see for instance
[
BGS94
, (1.4.10)]
).

We get:

\displaystyle\log|c_{\mathbf{n}}|-\log\|(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\|\leq

\displaystyle-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})-n\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log|z|\,\mu_{k}

(8.2.20)

\displaystyle+D\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\iota,\varphi_{r(n_{j})})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],g_{k})+o(D),

giving for any fixed
\gamma\geq 0
independent of
k
and
D
, and for all large enough
k\gg 1
:

\displaystyle\limsup_{\begin{subarray}{c}D\rightarrow\infty\\
|\mathbf{n}|\geq\gamma D\end{subarray}}^{\prime}\frac{h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})+\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})}{D}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}(\iota,\varphi_{r(n_{j})})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],g_{k})

(8.2.21)

\displaystyle-\gamma\left(\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|+\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log|z|\,\mu_{k}\right),

where the dash over the limit supremum indicates that we consider all

\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}
with
|\mathbf{n}|\geq\gamma D
and sharing some fixed
r(\mathbf{n})
(note that there are only
(l+1)^{d}
many possibilities of
r(\mathbf{n})
).
Here, remarking that
\log{|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|}>0
while the uniform limit

g_{k}\to\log^{+}|z|^{-1}
on
\overline{\mathbf{D}}
implies

\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log|z|\,\mu_{k}\to 0,

the meaning of “large enough
k
” is specified by the positivity of the term
\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|+\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log|z|\,\mu_{k}
.
At this point, the requisite estimate (
8.2.19
) follows
34
34

34

To be fully rigorous, the proof of (
8.2.19
) is completed by the paragraph below. We firstly note a mild sloppiness in our formulation due to the involvement of
\gamma
and the requirement to only work with the
\mathbf{n}
constrained by
|\mathbf{n}|/D\geq\gamma
. In practical terms, we use a large deviations bound to show that
|\mathbf{n}|/D=d(m/2+o(1))
for most
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}
, and take
\gamma=d(m/2-\epsilon_{0})
with
\epsilon_{0}\rightarrow 0
in the end. Then (
8.2.21
) is used for these
\mathbf{n}
, while a trivial estimate applies to the leftover meagre set of
\mathbf{n}
.
In any case, here is a rigorous completion of the proof of the requisite bound (
8.2.19
). For any
\epsilon>0
, we can pick
k\gg 1
(depending on
d
) such that
\int_{\overline{\mathbf{D}}}\log|z|\,\mu_{k}<\epsilon
, and
\left|(\iota,\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})})^{*}\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],g_{k})\right)-\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))\right|<\epsilon
. For this specific
k
, we consider
D\gg 1
such that the
o(D)
in (
8.2.20
) is
<\epsilon D/2
. From (
8.2.21
),

h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})\leq-n\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|-\sum_{j=1}^{d}n_{j}\log r(n_{j})+D\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))+\epsilon(n+D).

At this point (
8.2.19
) follows by Lemma
7.3.17
and Shidlovsky’s lemma, which give
n=|\mathbf{n}|=O(dD)
for
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}
.
form the convergence

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\left((\iota,\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})})^{*}\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot([0],g_{k})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\overline{\mathcal{O}(1)}\cdot(\iota,\varphi_{r(\mathbf{n})})_{*}([0],\log^{+}|z|^{-1})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi).

Armed with (
8.2.19
), and using inputs from the functional bad approximability theorems in §
3.2
(which are in place since, in all cases, the
f_{i}
are holonomic functions; noting that the proof of Lemma
7.3.17
entails automatic holonomicity from the condition
\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|>\sum_{h=1}^{r}\max_{1\leq i\leq m}b_{i,h}
), we now estimate the total contribution

\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbf{N}^{d}}\mathrm{rank}\,\left(E^{(\mathbf{n})}/E^{(\mathbf{n}^{+})}\right)\cdot h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})

of the archimedean height showing in the right-hand side of Bost’s slopes inequality (
7.2.14
).

For any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, Lemma
3.2.14
shows that all
D\gg_{\epsilon^{\prime},\{f_{i}\}}1
satisfy

\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\subset\left[0,(m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right]^{d}.

(In the Lemma, we may pick
\varepsilon:=\epsilon^{\prime}/2
and we consider
D\gg_{\epsilon^{\prime},\{f_{i}\}}1
such that
\epsilon^{\prime}D/2>C(\varepsilon)
.)
By (
8.2.19
), which by definition is an upper bound on the
\mathbf{n}^{\mathrm{th}}
archimedean evaluation height
h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{\mathbf{n}})
, we have:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})

(8.2.22)

\displaystyle\leq-\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|\left(\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}|\mathbf{n}|\right)+D\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(-\frac{n_{j}}{D}\log r(n_{j})+\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))\right)+o(D^{d+1}).

We estimate the quantities

\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}|\mathbf{n}|,\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}(n_{j}/D)\log r(n_{j}),\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))

using the following consequence of Theorem
4.2.1
, to the effect that most
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}
have uniformly distributed components. Similarly to §
6.2
, recall from the statement of Theorem
8.0.1
, we use
P_{\varepsilon}^{d}(N)\subset[0,N]^{d}\cap\mathbf{Z}^{d}
to denote the subset of those
\mathbf{n}
for which the normalized
\left([0,1),\mu_{\mathrm{Lebesgue}}\right)
discrepancy of
\{n_{i}/N\}_{i=1}^{d}
is
\leq\varepsilon
, and
B_{d}^{\varepsilon}(N)
to denote the complement of
P_{\varepsilon}^{d}(N)
in
[0,N]^{d}\cap\mathbf{Z}^{d}
.

Lemma 8.2.23
.

There is a function
c:(0,1)\to(0,1)
, such that the following holds.

Consider an
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\in(0,1)
.
Then, for all
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
small enough with respect to
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}
,

\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\left\{\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)\right\}}{\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}=O\left(e^{-c(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})d}\right),

where
the implicit coefficient is absolute.

Proof.

Note that
\#\left\{\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)\right\}\leq\#B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)
, and recall that

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}{m^{d}D^{d}}=\frac{\mathrm{rank}\,E_{D}}{m^{d}D^{d}}=1.

Hence, it suffices to show that
\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}{m^{d}D^{d}}=O(e^{-cd})
for some
c=c(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})>0
and a small enough
\epsilon^{\prime}
.

By Theorem
4.2.1
, we have

\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\#B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)/((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D)^{d}=O(e^{-c_{0}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})d})),

with a certain
c_{0}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})>0
and an absolute implicit coefficient.
For
\epsilon^{\prime}
sufficiently small in terms of
c_{0}(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})
, we will have
\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D)^{d}/(mD)^{d}=O(e^{c_{0}(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})d/2})
. We obtain the desired bound with
c:=c_{0}/2
.
∎

Now, for an arbitrary
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}>0
, we pick the sufficiently small
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
as guaranteed by Lemma
8.2.23
,
and apply Lemma
3.2.14
as discussed above to obtain that
\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\subset\left[0,(m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right]^{d}
for
D\gg 1
. We obtain:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}|\mathbf{n}|}{dm^{d}D^{d+1}}\right\}\geq\ \lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}|\mathbf{n}|}{dD\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{(\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right))(m+\epsilon^{\prime})(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}})/2}{\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(m+\epsilon^{\prime})(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}})/2.

Here, the second inequality follows upon remarking that the definition of the discrepancy function implies
|\mathbf{n}|\geq dD(m+\epsilon^{\prime})(1-2\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}})/2
for all
\mathbf{n}\in P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)
;

and the last equality follows from Lemma
8.2.23
which implies that, for fixed
\epsilon^{\prime\prime},\epsilon^{\prime}
,

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}{\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}=\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\left\{1-O(e^{-c(\epsilon^{\prime\prime})d})\right\}=1.

Now we let
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0
(this will force
\epsilon^{\prime}\rightarrow 0
). We get:

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}|\mathbf{n}|}{dm^{d}D^{d+1}}\right\}\geq\frac{m}{2}.

(8.2.24)

Similarly, still directly from the definition of the discrepancy function, we have the following evaluation for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left\{-(n_{j}/D)\log r(n_{j})+\widehat{T}\left(r(n_{j}),\varphi)\right)\right\}

(8.2.25)

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{m}\sum_{k=0}^{l}\left\{(\alpha_{k+1}-\alpha_{k})\widehat{T}\left(r_{k},\varphi\right)-\frac{1}{2}(\alpha_{k+1}^{2}-\alpha_{k}^{2})\log r_{k}\right\}+O\left(d(\epsilon^{\prime}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}})\right),

recalling the notations (
7.1.7
) and (
8.2.12
). (The implicit coefficient here depends linearly on
m|\log{r_{0}}|+\widehat{T}(1,\varphi)
.)
A partial summation now gives

\displaystyle\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(-(n_{j}/D)\log r(n_{j})+\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))\right)}{dm^{d}D^{d}}\right\}

(8.2.26)

\displaystyle=\widehat{T}(1,\varphi)-\frac{1}{2m}\sum_{k=1}^{l}\frac{(\widehat{T}(r_{k},\varphi)-\widehat{T}(r_{k-1},\varphi))^{2}}{\log{r_{k}}-\log{r_{k-1}}}+O((\epsilon^{\prime}+\sqrt{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}})),

and the last error term goes to
0
once we take
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\rightarrow 0
(which implies
\epsilon^{\prime}\to 0
).

Further, for all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}
, we certainly have the following trivial bound

\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(-(n_{j}/D)\log r(n_{j})+\widehat{T}\left(r(n_{j}),\varphi)\right)\right)\leq d(m+\epsilon^{\prime})|\log r_{0}|+d\widehat{T}(1,\varphi),

and thus

\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\left(-(n_{j}/D)\log r(n_{j})+\widehat{T}(r(n_{j}),\varphi))\right)}{dm^{d}D^{d}}=0.

(8.2.27)

Combining (
8.2.22
), (
8.2.24
), (
8.2.26
), and (
8.2.27
), we arrive at our total archimedean evaluation height bound:

\displaystyle\lim_{d\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle\lim_{D\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}}h_{\infty}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})}{dm^{d}D^{d+1}}\right\}

(8.2.28)

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{m}{2}\log|\varphi^{\prime}(0)|+\widehat{T}(1,\varphi)-\frac{1}{2m}\sum_{k=1}^{l}\frac{(\widehat{T}(r_{k},\varphi)-\widehat{T}(r_{k-1},\varphi))^{2}}{\log{r_{k}}-\log{r_{k-1}}}.

8.2.29.
Non-archimedean estimate

The main idea here is similar to §
6.6
.

Let
h_{\mathrm{fin}}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})
denote
\sum_{v\nmid\infty}h_{v}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})
. For
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}
and a prime
p
, by definition,
h_{p}(\psi_{D}^{(\mathbf{n})})
is
\log p
times the maximal
p
-adic valuation
v_{p,\mathbf{n}}
of the denominators of the (
\mathbf{n}-D
)-th coefficient of
\sum_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}f_{\mathbf{i}}Q_{\mathbf{i}}
across all
(Q_{\mathbf{i}})_{\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\in E_{D}
. Since all
Q_{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbf{x})
are
\mathbf{Z}
-linear combinations of monomials
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{k}}
with
\mathbf{k}\in[-D,0]^{d}
, it follows that
v_{p,\mathbf{n}}
is at most the maximum of the
p
-adic valuations of the denominators of the
\mathbf{x}^{\mathbf{m}}
coefficients of all
f_{\mathbf{i}}(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{m}
with
(n_{j}-D)^{+}\leq m_{j}\leq n_{j}
for all
1\leq j\leq d
; here for once we write
(n_{j}-D)^{+}:=\max(n_{j}-D,0)
.
We consider separately the
\prod_{h=1}^{r}[1,\ldots,b_{i,h}n]
and the
n^{e_{i}}
pieces of the
p
-denominators of the coefficients of
f_{i}(x)
:

v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\flat}:=\max_{\mathbf{m}\preceq\mathbf{n},\,\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)}\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{val}_{p}([1,\ldots,b_{i_{j},1}\cdot m_{j}]\cdots[1,\ldots,b_{i_{j},r}\cdot m_{j}])\right\},

v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\sharp}:=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{i}\in V_{m}^{d}(\epsilon)\\
(n_{j}-D)^{+}\leq m_{j}\leq n_{j},\forall 1\leq j\leq d\end{subarray}}\left\{\sum_{j=1}^{d}e_{i_{j}}\mathrm{val}_{p}(\max\{m_{j},1\})\right\},

where
\mathrm{val}_{p}
denotes the usual
p
-adic valuation with
\mathrm{val}_{p}(p)=1
.
Here we use the convention that for
m_{j}=0
, we set
[1,\ldots,b_{i_{j},h}\cdot m_{j}]=1
.
By definition, we have

v_{p,\mathbf{n}}\leq v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\flat}+v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\sharp}.

(8.2.30)

We continue with the notations from §
8.2.11
; in particular,
\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\subset[0,(m+\epsilon^{\prime})D]^{d}
by Lemma
3.2.14
. We firstly discuss
v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\flat}
.
For the case
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)
, we stick to the trivial bound. Observe that
\prod_{h=1}^{r}[1,\ldots,(\max_{1\leq i\leq m}b_{i,h})\cdot n]
is a multiple of the denominators of the
x^{n}
-coefficients of all
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}
.
By the prime number theorem, it follows that

\sum_{p}v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\flat}\log p\leq\left(\sum_{h=1}^{r}\max_{1\leq i\leq m}b_{i,h}\right)|\mathbf{n}|+o(|\mathbf{n}|).

(8.2.31)

Summing over all
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)
, so that in particular
|\mathbf{n}|\leq d(m+\epsilon^{\prime})D
, we have, as
d\rightarrow\infty
:

\displaystyle\limsup_{D\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}\sum_{p}v_{p,\mathbf{n}}^{\flat}\log p}{dm^{d}D^{d+1}}\right\}

(8.2.32)

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{D\rightarrow\infty}\left\{\frac{\left(\sum_{h=1}^{r}\max_{1\leq i\leq m}b_{i,h}\right)(m+\epsilon^{\prime})Dd}{dD}\frac{\#\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap B_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)}{m^{d}D^{d}}\right\}

\displaystyle=O\left(\left(\sum_{h=1}^{r}\max_{1\leq i\leq m}b_{i,h}\right)(m+\epsilon^{\prime})e^{-c(\varepsilon^{\prime\prime})d}\right)=o_{d\to\infty}(1).

For the case
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{V}_{D}^{d}\cap P_{d}^{\epsilon^{\prime\prime}}\left((m+\epsilon^{\prime})D\right)
: at a fixed
p
, our assumption on the denominator types of the
f_{i}
says that the
p
-adic valuation of the denominators of the coefficients of all the monomials with exponent vectors
\preceq\mathbf{n}
in
f_{\mathbf{i}}
is at most

\sum_{j=1}^{d}\mathrm{val}_{p}\left([1,\ldots,b_{i_{j},1}\cdot n_{j}]\cdots[1,\ldots,b_{i_{j},r}\cdot n_{j}]\right);

for
